## Checkpoint296 — Unbounded auxiliary mass and the polar metric

Example 15.6.8 on printed page 457 constructs a compact complex sixfold with a smooth strictly positive genuinely nonclosed background and unbounded bounded-potential mass. Two projective lines, the Hopf threefold and one further projective line form the product. A positive nonconstant coefficient h over the first two curves makes the vertical term nonclosed. Scaling the Hopf smooth potentials by c=min h preserves positivity; top-degree expansion gives the lower bound 120c^3 times their diverging threefold masses. The factor 120=6!/3! is explicit. The separated unit-divisor locus has a complete geodesic polar metric of diameter 4, closed ideal analytic closure and unique nearest-point 1-Lipschitz ideal retraction. Two analytic ideal models have polar distance 1, while every ordinary representative of each has zero extended mass. The product locus is explicit; the unrestricted topology problem is unchanged.

## Checkpoint294 — General auxiliary product models

Proposition 15.6.18 on page455 proves the following. Arbitrary compact complex auxiliary factors and smooth semipositive vertical terms preserve the separated curve envelope identities, complete geodesic polar metric, ideal analytic closure and nearest-point ideal retraction without an ambient mass bound. Zero-obstacle equality, atomic polar curvature, relative-envelope membership and equality at every smooth obstacle remain equivalent. All model comparison volumes vanish. Vanishing auxiliary terms or curve areas force nonbigness; positive curve areas and strictly positive vertical terms give bigness, including genuinely nonclosed examples. Genuine curve-product slices and countable-divisor domination compute the base projections. Unit-divisor reduction transfers them upstairs and gives zero mass for every ordinary representative. The common potential on Y times Z identifies the polar and actual analytic loci across auxiliary factors. Pullback of curve stretching competitors proves the obstacle criterion. Compact-fiber descent and closed top-degree auxiliary test forms prove the nonbig cases; smooth curve gauges prove bigness, and the mixed derivative component proves genuine nonclosedness.

## Checkpoint293 — All-obstacle separated product criterion

Lemma 15.6.6 on page452, Proposition 15.6.17 and Corollary 15.6.7 on page453 prove the following. Relative-envelope membership of a separated sum on a finite product is equivalent to membership of each coordinate in its own relative class. For arbitrary admissible curve backgrounds, zero-obstacle ordinary/ideal equality, equality of polar and atomic curvatures, relative membership in the ideal reference and equality at every smooth product obstacle are equivalent. Mixed obstacles, zero-area coordinates and zero-volume models are included. The same criterion holds through the nonnegative nonclosed auxiliary coefficient and unit-divisor extension. Add coordinate stretching competitors to obtain product membership; genuine coordinate restrictions and constant corrections give its converse. The separated zero-obstacle projections identify envelope equality factorwise, and the complete curve criterion and ideal obstacle reprojection give arbitrary mixed-obstacle equality. On the auxiliary extension, slices give the ordinary projection, preservation of ideals gives the ideal projection, and the same added competitors prove relative membership directly. A prose ending restores the missing automatic proof-ending symbol in the unchanged smooth geodesic equation.

## Checkpoint292 — Arbitrary curve product metric

Proposition 15.6.16 on page450 proves the following. The restricted unit-divisor product locus admits arbitrary smooth admissible curve backgrounds, including zero areas, with a complete geodesic sum polar metric, ideal analytic closure and unique nearest-point ideal retraction. Its diameter and bounded Hausdorff comparison are controlled exactly by curve areas in the ordered case. Area-decreasing maps are surjective ideal-commuting contractions with explicit lost-coordinate fiber diameters. Uniform zero-area degeneration converges in L1 to the singleton retaining the normalized smooth curve potentials. Separated curve projections and countable-divisor domination give the base model identities through every nonnegative auxiliary coefficient; unit-divisor reduction transfers them upstairs. Closed positive test forms prove nonbigness at zero auxiliary coefficient or any zero curve area. Curve polar classification and the weighted metric comparison transfer all metric statements. Actual rational atomic masses prove analytic density, while irrational area ratios need not preserve individual analytic models. Uniform curve zero-area collapse and fiber integration prove the product limit.

## Checkpoint291 — Separated curve obstacle projections

## Checkpoint290 — Weighted product metric comparison

Lemma 15.6.4 on page445 and Proposition 15.6.14 on page446 prove the following restricted results. The Hausdorff comparison defined through isometric embeddings has explicit parameter bounds for the bounded weighted ordinary and ideal model loci, even across different active sets. The comparison equals the difference of weighted areas for componentwise ordered coefficients, and equals the sum of weighted areas at the singleton limit. Coefficient-decreasing maps are surjective contractions commuting with ideal projection, with exact discarded-coordinate fiber diameters. Every locus with a positive curve coefficient is noncompact. A direct bridge metric on a disjoint union proves the correspondence-distortion upper bound without a compactness assumption. Diameter comparison gives the lower bound. Common model tuples give a correspondence with distortion controlled by the weighted curve areas; the two bounds agree for ordered coefficients. Coordinate uniqueness and polar-total-variation distances compute the transition maps and their fibers. Infinitely many separated atomic models prove failure of total boundedness. The unrestricted completion and degeneration problems in Chapters15 and32 remain open.

## Checkpoint228 — All smooth obstacles

Sections 15.6.4 and 29.12.3 develop convergence for every smooth obstacle. The resulting uniformity is covariant under smooth gauges and positive scaling, and its analytic closure is exactly ordinary/ideal envelope equality for all smooth obstacles. On curves this closure equals the zero-obstacle closure. The new pseudometric has complete canonical ideal loci, while the whole ordinary space remains incomplete even on a fixed positive-mass model family. On ordinary curve models its topology is strictly between the zero-obstacle topology and polar total variation.

## Checkpoint227 — Fixed-obstacle gauge discontinuity

Complete Chapter 15 is reconciled after the ordinary-type topology integration. Ideal quotient notation is separated from ordinary classes. ## Checkpoint226 — Ordinary singularity-type topology

Q4.1 is reconstructed in Chapters 1, 15 and 29. A general controlled-ideal estimate supports a uniformity on ordinary classes whose analytic closure and sequential closure are exactly envelope equality.
